=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a H \times W grid, and each cell contains 0 or 1. The cell at the i-th row from the top and the j-th column from the left contains an integer A_{i,j}.
You can perform the following two operations any number of times in any order:

- Operation X: Choose an integer x (1 \leq x \leq H). For every integer 1 \leq y \leq W, replace A_{x,y} with 1 - A_{x,y}.
- Operation Y: Choose an integer y (1 \leq y \leq W). For every integer 1 \leq x \leq H, replace A_{x,y} with 1 - A_{x,y}.

Find the minimum possible value of \displaystyle \sum_{x=1}^H\sum_{y=1}^W A_{x,y} after the process.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
H W
A_{1,1}A_{1,2}\ldots A_{1,W}
A_{2,1}A_{2,2}\ldots A_{2,W}
\vdots
A_{H,1}A_{H,2}\ldots A_{H,W}

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq H \leq 2\times 10^5
- 1 \leq W \leq 18
- H and W are integers.
- A_{i,1}A_{i,2}\ldots A_{i,W} is a length-W string consisting of 0 and 1.

Sample Input 1

3 3
100
010
110

Sample Output 1

2

By performing the following operations, the grid changes as shown below, and you get \displaystyle \sum_{x=1}^H\sum_{y=1}^W A_{x,y} = 2.

- Operation Y with y=1
- Operation X with x=2


It is impossible to make \displaystyle \sum_{x=1}^H\sum_{y=1}^W A_{x,y} \leq 1, so the answer is 2.

Sample Input 2

3 4
1111
1111
1111

Sample Output 2

0

Sample Input 3

10 5
10000
00111
11000
01000
10110
01110
10101
00100
00100
10001

Sample Output 3

13


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

def fwht(a):
    n=len(a)
    h=1
    while h<n:
        step = h << 1
        for i in range(0,n,step):
            for j in range(i,i+h):
                x = a[j]; y = a[j+h]
                a[j] = x + y
                a[j+h] = x - y
        h = step

This uses nested loops. For n=262144, 2.36M butterflies per transform. 7M, okay. But Python nested loops overhead? 7M inner iterations, each simple; maybe 1 sec? Actually 7M with list indexing maybe ~1s-2s. Good.

FWHT implementation details:
```
def fwht(a):
    n = len(a)
    h = 1
    while h < n:
        step = h << 1
        for i in range(0, n, step):
            for j in range(i, i + h):
                x = a[j]
                y = a[j + h]
                a[j] = x + y
                a[j + h] = x - y
        h = step
```

If c=1 as integer 1, bit 0 is last column, not first. But bit order doesn't matter as long as consistent? Let's examine. We represent row as int(s,2) where first char is most significant bit (bit W-1). If we choose c as integer with bit positions same as int(s,2), then c's bit W-1 corresponds to first column. When we enumerate all c from 0 to N-1, we enumerate all subsets of columns, regardless of which bit maps to which column. XOR operation and popcount are invariant under permuting bit positions. Since we enumerate all c, the set of c values covers all column flip subsets, but the mapping from integer to subset is a fixed bijection. The convolution result F(c) = Σ cnt[m] g(c xor m) depends only on the XOR of masks under that bijection. If we permute bit positions, cnt and g are permuted accordingly; XOR convolution result is permuted accordingly. The minimum over all c is invariant. So int(s,2) with MSB first is fine. Good.

Let's maybe write code:

```
import sys

def fwht(a):
    n = len(a)
    h = 1
    while h < n:
        step = h << 1
        for i in range(0, n, step):
            end = i + h
            for j in range(i, end):
                x = a[j]
                y = a[j + h]
                a[j] = x + y
                a[j + h] = x - y
        h = step

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    H, W = map(int, input().split())
    N = 1 << W
    cnt = [0] * N
    for _ in range(H):
        s = input().strip()
        cnt[int(s, 2)] += 1

    g = [0] * N
    for m in range(N):
        b = m.bit_count()
        if b > W - b:
            b = W - b
        g[m] = b

    fwht(cnt)
    fwht(g)
    for i in range(N):
        cnt[i] *= g[i]
    fwht(cnt)
    print(min(cnt) // N)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```

    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```

```
pc = [0] * N

    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)

    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the FWHT function if `a` is a list and we use `for j in range(i, i + h);`; if h is large, range object created. Fine.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the FWHT function if `a` is a list and we use `for j in range(i, i + h);`; if h is large, range object created. Fine.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

```
pc = [0] * N

g = [0] * N

    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the FWHT function if `a` is a list and we use `for j in range(i, i + h);`; if h is large, range object created. Fine.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the FWHT function if `a` is a list and we use `for j in range(i, i + h);`; if h is large, range object created. Fine.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the FWHT function if `a` is a list and we use `for j in range(i, i + h);`; if h is large, range object created. Fine.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. pc = [0] * N

```
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N

    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```

This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

```
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the FWHT function if `a` is a list and we use `for j in range(i, i + h);`; if h is large, range object created. Fine.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the FWHT function if `a` is a list and we use `for j in range(i, i + h);`; if h is large, range object created. Fine.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the FWHT function if `a` is a list and we use `for j in range(i, i + h);`; if h is large, range object created. Fine.

Potential issue: The FWHT function as written uses `for i in range(0, n, step):` and inside `for j in range(i, i + h):`. For each i, `range(i, i+h)` creates a range object. Number of range objects per stage = n/step. Total range objects across all stages = sum n/step for step=2,4,...,n = n/2 + n/4 + ... +1 = n-1 ~262k. That's okay. But creating 262k range objects might add overhead. Could use while loops to avoid? Actually for loops with range create range object per i, but range object creation is cheap. 262k range objects maybe not too bad. Could optimize by using while loop:
```
for i in range(0, n, step):
    j = i
    end = i + h
    while j < end:
        ...
        j += 1
```
This avoids range object per i but while loop in Python may be slower than for range. Which is faster? For small inner loops, for range is usually faster than while. Range object creation per i is C-level and cheap. 262k range objects maybe okay. Let's estimate: stages 18, total i iterations = sum n/step = n-1 = 262143. So 262k range objects. Not huge. Inner loop iterations 2.36M. Fine.

Could optimize by using `for j in range(i, i + h):` and `y = a[j + h];` okay.

Potential issue: If using PyPy, range object creation maybe different. Still okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if `N` is 262144 and `g` list comprehension uses `m.bit_count()`; bit_count is method call per iteration. 262k method calls, okay. Could use `int.bit_count(m)`? No. Method call overhead okay. If concerned, precompute popcounts via DP:
```
pc = [0] * N
for i in range(1, N):
    pc[i] = pc[i >> 1] + (i & 1)
g = [0] * N
for i in range(N):
    b = pc[i]
    g[i] = b if b <= W - b else W - b
```
This uses Python loop 262k for pc and 262k for g, total 524k iterations, maybe similar to bit_count method calls. bit_count is C, likely faster. Use bit_count.

```
        j += 1
```

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with using `min(cnt) // N` if `cnt` after inverse
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def fwht(a):
    n = len(a)
    h = 1
    while h < n:
        step = h << 1
        for i in range(0, n, step):
            for j in range(i, i + h):
                x = a[j]
                y = a[j + h]
                a[j] = x + y
                a[j + h] = x - y
        h = step

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    H, W = map(int, input().split())
    N = 1 << W

    cnt = [0] * N
    for _ in range(H):
        s = input().strip()
        cnt[int(s, 2)] += 1

    g = [0] * N
    for m in range(N):
        b = m.bit_count()
        if b > W - b:
            b = W - b
        g[m] = b

    fwht(cnt)
    fwht(g)

    for i in range(N):
        cnt[i] *= g[i]

    fwht(cnt)

    print(min(cnt) // N)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
